```json
  "old_string": "\\paragraph{Why does scrambled equal correct?}\nScrambled labels match correct labels within 3.9\\,pp across all OOD conditions. Four post-hoc hypotheses may explain this: (1) \\emph{regularization equivalence}---any multi-class cross-entropy loss regularizes representations comparably, regardless of label semantics; (2) \\emph{gradient diversity}---random 9-class targets distribute gradients across more output dimensions than binary, providing representational breadth independent of correctness; (3) \\emph{domain exposure dominance}---the classification structure forces the encoder to attend to domain-relevant tokens, and the resulting domain exposure dominates over label-specific information; (4) \\emph{ceiling effect}---the downstream BiGRU has sufficient capacity to compensate for noisy Stage-1 representations when domain exposure is held constant. Distinguishing these hypotheses requires targeted experiments (e.g., varying the number of random classes while holding domain exposure constant) beyond this work's scope.",
  "new_string": "\\paragraph{Why does scrambled equal correct?}\nScrambled labels match correct labels within 3.9\\,pp. Post-hoc hypotheses include regularization equivalence (any multi-class cross-entropy provides comparable representational structure), gradient diversity from 9 vs.\\ 2 output classes, and domain exposure dominance---the classification structure forces attention to domain-relevant tokens regardless of label semantics. Distinguishing these requires varying class count while holding domain exposure constant."
```
```json
}
```

```json
  "old_string": "\\paragraph{Encoder capacity confound.}\nOur three encoders (125M--355M) span a 2.8$\\times$ capacity range but also differ in pretraining and tokenization; the capacity-dependent observation (Section~\\ref{sec:cross_encoder}) requires same-architecture scaling to confirm. RoBERTa-large achieves F1\\,=\\,1.000 on ActorAttack without adaptation, but this may reflect its 355M capacity, its pretraining corpus, or both; same-architecture scaling (e.g., DeBERTa-base vs.\\ DeBERTa-large) would be needed to attribute this result to capacity alone.\n\n\\paragraph{Temporal modeling.}\nMean pooling matches BiGRU performance (Section~\\ref{sec:decomposition}), indicating that conversation-level detection relies on turn-level features rather than their sequential order. This is consistent with the persuasion-as-domain-shift hypothesis: if domain adaptation shifts individual turn representations into a separable region, temporal aggregation strategy is secondary.",
  "new_string": "\\paragraph{Encoder capacity and temporal modeling.}\nOur three encoders (125M--355M) span a 2.8$\\times$ capacity range but also differ in pretraining and tokenization; same-architecture scaling is needed to confirm the capacity-dependent observation. Mean pooling matches BiGRU (Section~\\ref{sec:decomposition}), suggesting detection relies on turn-level features rather than sequential order---consistent with domain adaptation shifting individual representations into a separable region."
```
```json
}
```

```json
  "old_string": "Both training and synthetic OOD data are generated by Qwen3-8B. {
```
```json
  "replace_all": "False",
  "file_path": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_2/projects/persuasion_turn_jailbreak_detect/docs/paper/conclusion.tex",
  "old_string": "On synthetic multi-turn data generated by a single LLM, three findings emerge. First, for base-size encoders (125--184M), classification-based DAPT is the most robust adaptation; unsupervised pretraining closes $\\sim$60\\,pp on single-source data but collapses on mixed-LLM training, while the largest encoder (355M) generalizes without adaptation. Second, fewer attacks require adaptation as model size grows. Third, a variance reduction of 4--20$\\times$ is observed (3 seeds) for classification supervision on synthetic data, but this reverses on human data (ToxicChat MLM F1 0.837 vs.\\ 9-class 0.596).\nThree counter-intuitive negative results---label correctness does not affect detection, temporal order is irrelevant, and domain specificity is marginal---together constrain which properties of domain adaptation matter for cross-attack generalization.\nMulti-turn human evaluation and LLM-scale (7B+) encoder adaptation remain the key open directions. These findings suggest that lightweight continued pretraining offers a practical alternative to LLM-as-judge approaches for real-time multi-turn safety monitoring.",
  "new_string": "On synthetic multi-turn data generated by a single LLM, classification-based DAPT is the most robust adaptation for base-size encoders; unsupervised pretraining closes $\\sim$60\\,pp on single-source data but collapses on mixed-LLM training. Fewer attacks require adaptation as model size grows, and a variance reduction of 4--20$\\times$ is observed (3 seeds) on synthetic data but reverses on human data.\nThree negative results---scrambled labels match correct, mean pooling matches BiGRU, and domain specificity is marginal---constrain which adaptation properties matter for cross-attack generalization. Lightweight continued pretraining offers a practical alternative to LLM-as-judge approaches for real-time multi-turn safety monitoring."
```
```json
}
```

### Tool result

_source line 6769 · 2026-05-20T09:21:24.885417+00:00_

/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_2/projects/persuasion_turn_jailbreak_detect/docs/paper/introduction.tex:12:We test this hypothesis through controlled ablation. Our pipeline, \textbf{DisPeD} (Disentangled Persuasion Detection), separates encoder adaptation (Stage~1) from temporal classification (Stage~2, a frozen BiGRU; Figure~\ref{fig:architecture}). Seven Stage~1 variants each isolate a single factor (e.g., domain exposure, label quality, temporal modeling) while holding all other components constant (Section~\ref{sec:method}). The contribution is the systematic decomposition of what enables cross-attack generalization, not the pipeline itself.
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_2/projects/persuasion_turn_jailbreak_detect/docs/paper/appendix.tex:218:Table~\ref{tab:cross_llm} shows three inference conditions. When both jailbreak and benign text come from Llama (row~2), all variants collapse (F1 = 0.26--0.43), but this conflates two sources of distribution shift: unfamiliar jailbreak patterns and unfamiliar benign text. Row~3 isolates jailbreak transferability by pairing Llama jailbreaks with the original Qwen benign distribution. Under this condition, 9-class recovers to F1 = 1.000 on DD and 0.801 on ActorAttack, and JB-MLM reaches 0.903 on ActorAttack. The contrast between rows~2 and~3 shows that the dominant failure mode in row~2 is benign distribution mismatch (false positives from unfamiliar benign text), not inability to recognize cross-LLM jailbreak patterns. Vanilla remains at 0.26--0.31 across all conditions, further confirming that encoders without DAPT lack OOD detection capability regardless of the LLM source. The residual degradation on ActorAttack (0.801 vs.\ 0.972) reflects genuine cross-LLM variation in structurally complex attack patterns.

3	\section{Discussion}
4	\label{sec:discussion}

6	\paragraph{Topic shortcut confound.}
7	Three observations argue against a topic-level shortcut: (1) topic classification yields unstable OOD generalization (F1\,=\,0.541$\pm$0.207 on DD); (2) scrambled labels match correct labels (F1 gap $\leq$3.9\,pp); (3) MLM detects without labels (F1\,$\geq$\,0.97 at $K \leq 5$). Domain exposure, not topic classification, drives detection.
8	
9	\paragraph{Single-LLM confound.}
10	Both training and synthetic OOD data are generated by Qwen3-8B. ToxicChat confirms transfer to human data (+18--42\,pp; Table~\ref{tab:human_data}); cross-LLM experiments validate transfer when the benign distribution is held constant (Appendix~\ref{app:cross_llm}).
11	Mixed-LLM 9-class maintains DD F1\,=\,0.818$\pm$0.096 (+53\,pp over vanilla) while MLM performs at vanilla level, but AA degrades (0.504$\pm$0.107). This is condition-specific: single-source training achieves AA F1\,$\geq$\,0.91; the gap likely reflects limited per-source volume (250 per LLM).
12	
13	\paragraph{Variance reversal on human data.}
14	On synthetic OOD, classification supervision reduces variance 4--20$\times$ (3 seeds) over MLM; on ToxicChat this reverses (std\,=\,0.087 vs.\ 0.013), suggesting the benefit is condition-specific.
15	
16	\paragraph{Why does scrambled equal correct?}
17	Scrambled labels match correct labels within 3.9\,pp. Post-hoc hypotheses include regularization equivalence (any multi-class cross-entropy provides comparable structure), gradient diversity from 9 vs.\ 2 classes, and domain exposure dominance. Distinguishing these requires varying class count while holding domain exposure constant.
18	
19	\paragraph{Encoder capacity and temporal modeling.}

1	% abstract.tex — D048 deep writing optimization
2	\begin{abstract}
3	Detecting unseen multi-turn jailbreaks requires domain adaptation, but the form of adaptation matters.
4	Multi-turn jailbreak attacks spread adversarial intent across benign-seeming turns, and existing defenses fail to generalize across attack families.
5	We disentangle the factors behind cross-attack generalization on synthetic multi-turn data through controlled ablation: thirteen encoder configurations at three scales (125 to 355M parameters) disentangle the contributions of domain exposure, classification supervision, label quality, and granularity on three out-of-distribution (OOD) attack families and two training compositions.
6	On single-source synthetic data, continued pretraining closes roughly 60 percentage points of the OOD gap without labels. This effectiveness is condition-dependent: on mixed-LLM training data, unsupervised pretraining collapses while classification-based adaptation remains the strongest variant (DD OOD F1\,=\,0.818 vs.\ 0.291 without adaptation).
7	A capacity-dependent observation holds across scales: as model size grows, the set of attacks requiring adaptation shrinks, until the largest encoder generalizes without adaptation.
8	A variance reduction of 4--20$\times$ is observed across 3 seeds for classification supervision on synthetic evaluations regardless of label correctness, but this benefit reverses on human-authored data.
9	These findings show that the adaptation mechanism, not just domain exposure, determines cross-condition robustness in multi-turn jailbreak detection.
10	\end{abstract}
11

3	\section{Experiments}
4	\label{sec:experiments}

6	\subsection{Setup}
7	\label{sec:setup}
8	
9	\paragraph{Data.}
10	All synthetic data is generated by Qwen3-8B~\citep{qwen3_2025}.
11	The IID dataset contains 250 Crescendo~\citep{russinovich2024crescendo} jailbreak and 250 topic-anchored benign conversations, split into training (350), validation (74), and test (76) sets with balanced class ratios (e.g., test = 38 jailbreak + 38 benign).
12	Stage~1 (DAPT) uses training + validation conversations; Stage~2 (BiGRU) trains on training, early-stops on validation, and evaluates on test.
13	OOD evaluation pairs attack-specific jailbreak conversations with the 38 IID-test benign conversations: 80 FITD~\citep{fitd2024} + 38 benign = 118 total, 80 Deceptive Delight (DD)~\citep{deceptivedelight2024} + 38 benign = 118 total, and 80 ActorAttack~\citep{ren2024actorattack} + 38 benign = 118 total.
14	No overlap exists in attack templates across families.
15	We intentionally hold the benign distribution constant across OOD evaluations to isolate attack-side generalization; the effect of varying the benign distribution is measured independently in cross-LLM experiments (Appendix~\ref{app:cross_llm}), where replacing Qwen benign text with Llama benign text increases false-positive rates from 0\% to 88--90\% while true-positive rates remain constant (Table~\ref{tab:cross_llm_error}).
16	A paraphrased version of the IID test set (BERTScore 0.92, lexical overlap 0.20) serves as the adversarial robustness benchmark (Section~\ref{sec:perturbation}).
17	For human data validation, we use ToxicChat~\citep{lin2023toxicchat} (204 jailbreak + 204 benign from real LMSYS user interactions), entirely independent of Qwen3-8B generation.
18	
19	\paragraph{Models.}
20	Seven DeBERTa-v3 variants share the same Stage-2 pipeline and differ only in Stage-1 training (Section~\ref{sec:method}).

1	% related_work.tex — D048 deep writing optimization
2	
3	\section{Related Work}
4	\label{sec:related_work}
5	
6	\paragraph{Multi-turn jailbreak attacks.}
7	All multi-turn jailbreak families share a persuasion substrate~\citep{zeng2024pap,hagendorff2026reasoning,li2024mhj}, yet each operationalizes it differently: Crescendo~\citep{russinovich2024crescendo} gradually escalates, FITD~\citep{fitd2024} exploits commitment-consistency bias, DD~\citep{deceptivedelight2024} combines emotional manipulation with role assignment, ActorAttack~\citep{ren2024actorattack} steers through multiple personas, and automated methods (PAIR~\citep{chao2023pair}, TAP~\citep{mehrotra2024tap}) iteratively refine adversarial prompts. \citet{wei2023jailbroken} provide a unifying taxonomy; benchmarks~\citep{mazeika2024harmbench,chao2024jailbreakbench} standardize single-turn comparison, while MultiBreak~\citep{song2026multibreak} targets multi-turn evaluation. This shared substrate with diverse surface strategies defines our evaluation: a detector must generalize across families, not merely succeed on its training distribution.
8	
9	\paragraph{Multi-turn defenses and production safety.}
10	No existing defense evaluates cross-attack transfer. Recent conversation-level systems pair fine-tuned encoders with sequence-level aggregation via GRU hierarchies (DeepContext~\citep{deepcontext2026}), turn-intention inference (BIID~\citep{tong2025biid}), graph neural networks (G-Guard~\citep{gguard2025}), or predictive generation (SafeDream~\citep{safedream2026}), yet each evaluates on a single attack family. Representation-level approaches include X-Boundary~\citep{lu2025xboundary} for multi-turn safety boundaries, white-box internal analysis~\citep{kadali2026trace}, and vision-language detection~\citep{hua2026rcs}; our work uses black-box classification. Per-turn classifiers (Llama Guard~\citep{inan2023llamaguard,metallamaguard3_2024}, ShieldGemma~\citep{zeng2024shieldgemma}, WildGuard~\citep{han2024wil
…[truncated 2473 chars]

_source line 6791 · 2026-05-20T09:23:00.396881+00:00_

1	% abstract.tex — D048 deep writing optimization
2	\begin{abstract}
3	Detecting unseen multi-turn jailbreaks requires domain adaptation, but the form of adaptation matters.
4	Multi-turn jailbreak attacks spread adversarial intent across benign-seeming turns, and existing defenses fail to generalize across attack families.
5	We disentangle the factors behind cross-attack generalization on synthetic multi-turn data through controlled ablation: thirteen encoder configurations at three scales (125 to 355M parameters) disentangle the contributions of domain exposure, classification supervision, label quality, and granularity on three out-of-distribution (OOD) attack families and two training compositions.
6	On single-source synthetic data, continued pretraining closes roughly 60 percentage points of the OOD gap without labels. This effectiveness is condition-dependent: on mixed-LLM training data, unsupervised pretraining collapses while classification-based adaptation remains the strongest variant (DD OOD F1\,=\,0.818 vs.\ 0.291 without adaptation).
7	A capacity-dependent observation holds across scales: as model size grows, the set of attacks requiring adaptation shrinks, until the largest encoder generalizes without adaptation.
8	A variance reduction of 4--20$\times$ is observed across 3 seeds for classification supervision on synthetic evaluations regardless of label correctness, but this benefit reverses on human-authored data.
9	These findings show that the adaptation mechanism, not just domain exposure, determines cross-condition robustness in multi-turn jailbreak detection.
10	\end{abstract}
11

1	% conclusion.tex — compressed for 8-page limit
2	
3	\section{Discussion}
4	\label{sec:discussion}
5	
6	\paragraph{Topic shortcut confound.}
7	Three observations argue against a topic-level shortcut: (1) topic classification yields unstable OOD generalization (F1\,=\,0.541$\pm$0.207 on DD); (2) scrambled labels match correct labels (F1 gap $\leq$3.9\,pp); (3) MLM detects without labels (F1\,$\geq$\,0.97 at $K \leq 5$). Domain exposure, not topic classification, drives detection.
8	
9	\paragraph{Single-LLM confound.}
10	Both training and synthetic OOD data are generated by Qwen3-8B. ToxicChat confirms transfer to human data (+18--42\,pp; Table~\ref{tab:human_data}); cross-LLM experiments validate transfer when the benign distribution is held constant (Appendix~\ref{app:cross_llm}).
11	Mixed-LLM 9-class maintains DD F1\,=\,0.818$\pm$0.096 (+53\,pp over vanilla) while MLM performs at vanilla level, but AA degrades (0.504$\pm$0.107). This is condition-specific: single-source training achieves AA F1\,$\geq$\,0.91; the gap likely reflects limited per-source volume (250 per LLM).
12	
13	\paragraph{Variance reversal on human data.}
14	On synthetic OOD, classification supervision reduces variance 4--20$\times$ (3 seeds) over MLM; on ToxicChat this reverses (std\,=\,0.087 vs.\ 0.013), suggesting the benefit is condition-specific.
15	
16	\paragraph{Why does scrambled equal correct?}
17	Scrambled labels match correct labels within 3.9\,pp. Post-hoc hypotheses include regularization equivalence (any multi-class cross-entropy provides comparable structure), gradient diversity from 9 vs.\ 2 classes, and domain exposure dominance. Distinguishing these requires varying class count while holding domain exposure constant.
18	
19	\paragraph{Encoder capacity and temporal modeling.}
20	Our three encoders (125M--355M) span a 2.8$\times$ capacity range but differ in pretraining and tokenization; same-architecture scaling is needed to confirm. Mean pooling matches BiGRU (Section~\ref{sec:d

Based on the search results, here's what I found about arXiv 2605.03095:

The paper titled "Revisiting JBShield: Breaking and Rebuilding Representation-Level Jailbreak Defenses" was submitted on May 4, 2026, with authors Kemal Derya and Berk Sunar.

**Overview:**
The paper addresses the challenge of defending large language models (LLMs) against jailbreak attacks, such as Greedy Coordinate Gradient (GCG), particularly under adaptive threat models where an attacker directly targets the defense mechanism.

**Main Findings:**
JBShield is a recent jailbreak defense with a 0% attack success rate in some settings that detects malicious prompts via two concept signals, a toxic concept and a jailbreak concept. However, the authors design JB-GCG, which modifies GCG's objective to combine two terms: refusal-direction suppression via cosine similarity between the refusal direction and hidden-state representations, and toxic-concept regularization via JBShield's own toxic concept score.

**Results:**
Across five configurations on Llama-3-8

I found the paper you're looking for! Here's what I found about arXiv 2604.28129:

## Latent Adversarial Detection: Adaptive Probing of LLM Activations for Multi-Turn Attack Detection

The paper proposes a defense that operates by probing the target LLM's internal activations to detect adversarial intent in how the model understands an incoming interaction, rather than what the attacker wrote.

**Key Findings:**

Multi-turn prompt injection follows a known attack path of trust-building, pivoting, and escalation. The research shows this attack path leaves an activation-level signature in the model's residual stream, where each phase shift moves the activation and produces a total path length far exceeding benign conversations, which the

[Director 指令] [Patch] Turn-shuffle ablation encoder 修正 + 跨 encoder 结果补充

**背景**：Reviewer 发现原 shuffle ablation 使用的是 RoBERTa-large (exp15) checkpoint，非 DeBERTa-v3。现已补跑 DeBERTa-v3 9-class shuffle，结果确认 order-invariance 跨 encoder 成立。

**需要修改**：

1. **Shuffle 结果表/段落**：原来只有一组数据，现在改为跨 encoder 对比表：

| Encoder | DD OOD Δ | AA OOD Δ | IID Δ |
|---------|----------|----------|-------|
| DeBERTa-v3-base | 0.000 | -0.009 | 0.000 |
| RoBERTa-large | +0.001 | -0.007 | 0.000 |

2. **文字表述**更新为跨 encoder 确认版本："Turn-order shuffling produces negligible performance change (|Δ| < 1pp) across both DeBERTa-v3-base and RoBERTa-large encoders, confirming that detection relies on per-turn representations rather than sequential patterns."

3. **确保不再将 RoBERTa-large 的 shuffle 数字误标为 DeBERTa-v3**

4. vanilla 的 shuffle 仅在 RoBERTa-large 上做了（DeBERTa vanilla DD OOD=0.312 有 floor effect），如果论文提到 vanilla shuffle，标明仅限 RoBERTa-large。

这次不用验证引用。页数预算注意保持 ≤8 页正文。

3	\section{Experiments}
4	\label{sec:experiments}
5	
6	\subsection{Setup}
7	\label{sec:setup}
8	
9	\paragraph{Data.}
10	All synthetic data is generated by Qwen3-8B~\citep{qwen3_2025}.
11	The IID dataset contains 250 Crescendo~\citep{russinovich2024crescendo} jailbreak and 250 topic-anchored benign conversations, split into training (350), validation (74), and test (76) sets with balanced class ratios (e.g., test = 38 jailbreak + 38 benign).
12	Stage~1 (DAPT) uses training + validation conversations; Stage~2 (BiGRU) trains on training, early-stops on validation, and evaluates on test.
13	OOD evaluation pairs attack-specific jailbreak conversations with the 38 IID-test benign conversations: 80 FITD~\citep{fitd2024} + 38 benign = 118 total, 80 Deceptive Delight (DD)~\citep{deceptivedelight2024} + 38 benign = 118 total, and 80 ActorAttack~\citep{ren2024actorattack} + 38 benign = 118 total.
14	No overlap exists in attack templates across families.
15	The OOD benign set is IID (same generation pipeline as training); the OOD challenge is in distinguishing novel attack families from familiar benign conversations.
16	A paraphrased version of the IID test set (BERTScore 0.92, lexical overlap 0.20) serves as the adversarial robustness benchmark (Section~\ref{sec:perturbation}).
17	For human data validation, we use ToxicChat~\citep{lin2023toxicchat} (204 jailbreak + 204 benign from real LMSYS user interactions), entirely independent of Qwen3-8B generation.
18	
19	\paragraph{Models.}
20	Seven DeBERTa-v3 variants share the same Stage-2 pipeline and differ only in Stage-1 training (Section~\ref{sec:method}).
21	Our vanilla DeBERTa+BiGRU configuration is architecturally equivalent to DeepContext~\citep{deepcontext2026}, differing only in encoder choice (DeBERTa-v3-base vs.\ BERT); it serves as our primary baseline.
22	For cross-encoder validation, we replicate vanilla, MLM, and 9-class variants on RoBERTa-base~\citep{liu2019
…[truncated 18773 chars]

5	\section{LLM-as-Judge Baseline}
6	\label{sec:llm_judge}
7	
8	\begin{table}[t]

11	\caption{LLM-as-judge jailbreak detection. IID: binary F1 (Crescendo test set); OOD: macro F1 (DD = Deceptive Delight, AA = ActorAttack). GPT-5.5 F1 values exclude unparseable responses and represent upper bounds.}

29	
30	Table~\ref{tab:llm_judge} reports LLM-as-judge performance on IID and OOD data.
31	On IID Crescendo, GPT-5.5 achieves precision of 1.0 at all turn prefixes but recall ranges from only 0.105 to 0.290, resulting in F1 = 0.383 on full conversations; 30--47\% of responses are unparseable.
32	Qwen3-8B performs worse (IID F1 = 0.100).
33	On OOD data, zero-shot Qwen3-8B achieves macro F1 = 0.244 on both DD and ActorAttack by predicting nearly all conversations as benign.
34	Even with 3-shot prompting, Qwen3-8B reaches only 0.566 (DD) and 0.652 (AA), while DisPeD maintains macro F1 $\geq$ 0.972.
35	The poor performance of content-level reasoning on unseen attack types underscores the necessity of trained classifiers with domain-adapted representations.

1	% method.tex — D024 compressed
2	
3	\section{Method}
4	\label{sec:method}
5	
6	Cross-attack generalization could stem from domain exposure, classification supervision, label correctness, label granularity, or domain specificity. Disentangling these factors demands an architecture where exactly one component varies across conditions. DisPeD separates encoder adaptation (Stage~1) from temporal classification (Stage~2). Seven Stage-1 variants share the same training data and downstream pipeline but differ solely in their pretraining objective; each isolates a single factor (Figure~\ref{fig:architecture}).
7	
8	\subsection{Problem Formulation}
9	
10	A conversation $C = \{u_1, a_1, \ldots, u_N, a_N\}$ alternates user turns $u_i$ and assistant responses $a_i$. Given a prefix of $K$ user turns, the model predicts $y \in \{0, 1\}$ (jailbreak vs.\ benign). We restrict input to user turns because adversarial intent resides in the attacker's messages. This formulation supports early detection from partial prefixes.
11	
12	\subsection{Stage 1: Turn-Level Encoder Adaptation}
13	\label{sec:stage1}
14	
15	Each user turn $u_i$ is encoded by DeBERTa-v3-base~\citep{he2023debertav3}; the \texttt{[CLS]} embedding $h_i \in \mathbb{R}^d$ serves as the turn representation. Seven variants, summarized in Table~\ref{tab:variants}, share the same training data but differ in objective.
16	
17	\begin{table}[t]
18	\centering
19	\small
20	\caption{Stage-1 encoder variants. All operate on the same training conversations except Wikipedia MLM (matched token count on Wikipedia text).}

2	
3	\section{Discussion}
4	\label{sec:discussion}
5	
6	\paragraph{Topic shortcut confound.}
7	Three observations argue against a topic-level shortcut: (1) topic classification yields unstable OOD generalization (F1\,=\,0.541$\pm$0.207 on DD); (2) scrambled labels match correct labels (F1 gap $\leq$3.9\,pp); (3) MLM detects without labels (F1\,$\geq$\,0.97 at $K \leq 5$). Domain exposure, not topic classification, drives detection.
8	
9	\paragraph{Single-LLM confound.}
10	Both training and synthetic OOD data are generated by Qwen3-8B. ToxicChat confirms transfer to human data (+18--42\,pp; Table~\ref{tab:human_data}); cross-LLM experiments validate transfer when the benign distribution is held constant (Appendix~\ref{app:cross_llm}).
11	Mixed-LLM 9-class maintains DD F1\,=\,0.818$\pm$0.096 (+53\,pp over vanilla) while MLM performs at vanilla level, but AA degrades (0.504$\pm$0.107). This is condition-specific: single-source training achieves AA F1\,$\geq$\,0.91; the gap likely reflects limited per-source volume (250 per LLM).
12	
13	\paragraph{Variance reversal on human data.}
14	On synthetic OOD, classification supervision reduces variance 4--20$\times$ (3 seeds) over MLM; on ToxicChat this reverses (std\,=\,0.087 vs.\ 0.013), suggesting the benefit is condition-specific.
15	
16	\paragraph{Why does scrambled equal correct?}
17	Scrambled labels match correct labels within 3.9\,pp. Post-hoc hypotheses include regularization equivalence (any multi-class cross-entropy provides comparable structure), gradient diversity from 9 vs.\ 2 classes, and domain exposure dominance. Distinguishing these requires varying class count while holding domain exposure constant.
18	
19	\paragraph{Encoder capacity and temporal modeling.}
20	Our three encoders (125M--355M) span a 2.8$\times$ capacity range but differ in pretraining and tokenization; same-architecture scaling is needed to confirm. Mean pooling matches BiGRU (Section~\ref{sec:d
…[truncated 9144 chars]

_source line 6851 · 2026-05-20T09:35:00.337912+00:00_

1	% abstract.tex — D048 deep writing optimization
2	\begin{abstract}
3	Detecting unseen multi-turn jailbreaks requires domain adaptation, but the form of adaptation matters.
4	Multi-turn jailbreak attacks spread adversarial intent across benign-seeming turns, and existing defenses fail to generalize across attack families.
5	We isolate the factors behind cross-attack generalization on synthetic multi-turn data through controlled ablation: thirteen encoder configurations at three scales (125 to 355M parameters) decompose the contributions of domain exposure, classification supervision, label quality, and granularity on three out-of-distribution (OOD) attack families and two training compositions.
6	On single-source synthetic data, continued pretraining closes roughly 60 percentage points of the OOD gap without labels. This effectiveness is condition-dependent: on mixed-LLM training data, unsupervised pretraining collapses while classification-based adaptation remains the strongest variant (DD OOD F1\,=\,0.818 vs.\ 0.291 without adaptation).
7	A capacity-dependent observation holds across scales: as model size grows, the set of attacks requiring adaptation shrinks, until the largest encoder generalizes without adaptation.
8	Classification supervision reduces prediction variance 4--20$\times$ on synthetic evaluations (observed across 3 seeds) regardless of label correctness, but this benefit reverses on human-authored data.
9	These findings show that the adaptation mechanism, not just domain exposure, determines cross-condition robustness in multi-turn jailbreak detection.
10	\end{abstract}
11

1	% introduction.tex — expanded for 8-page target
2	
3	\section{Introduction}
4	\label{sec:introduction}
5	
6	Multi-turn jailbreak attacks distribute adversarial intent across individually benign turns. Crescendo~\citep{russinovich2024crescendo}, Foot-in-the-Door~\citep{fitd2024}, and Deceptive Delight~\citep{deceptivedelight2024} all rely on persuasion as their core mechanism: \citet{zeng2024pap} catalogue 40 persuasion techniques across jailbreak families, and \citet{hagendorff2026reasoning} identify strategies in 97\% of successful attacks. Because individual attack turns occupy the same embedding region as benign text~\citep{bullwinkel2025repeng}, no single-turn filter can distinguish them from legitimate conversation.
7	
8	Existing defenses leave a critical gap. Production classifiers such as Llama Guard~\citep{inan2023llamaguard}, WildGuard~\citep{han2024wildguard}, and ShieldGemma~\citep{zeng2024shieldgemma} operate on individual turns and therefore miss manipulation that unfolds across exchanges. Multi-turn defenses have begun to model conversation-level structure: DeepContext~\citep{deepcontext2026} pairs fine-tuned BERT with a multi-layer GRU, BIID~\citep{tong2025biid} infers turn-level intentions, and G-Guard~\citep{gguard2025} applies graph neural networks. These systems achieve strong in-distribution performance, but each is evaluated on a single attack family. None answers the central question: \emph{what representation properties enable a detector to generalize across attack families it has never seen?}
9	
10	The simplest hypothesis is that pretrained language understanding already suffices. It does not, at least at base model scale. A vanilla DeBERTa-v3~\citep{he2021deberta} with temporal aggregation (cf.\ DeepContext) drops from F1\,=\,1.000 in-distribution to 0.312 on out-of-distribution (OOD) Deceptive Delight. LLM-as-judge approaches fare no better: GPT-5.5 reaches only 0.191--0.449 F1 with 30--47\% unparseable responses (Appendix~\ref{sec:llm_ju
…[truncated 3422 chars]

130	\vspace{2pt}
131	
132	TF-IDF collapses from 0.961 (DD) to 0.261 (ActorAttack, precision = 1.0, recall = 0.15). This gap shows that domain-adapted representations capture features beyond bag-of-words.
133	All classification-supervised variants converge to equivalent performance (0.951--0.990, max gap 3.9\,pp); this extends the DD finding. DAPT provides $\sim$71\,pp over both vanilla (0.258) and TF-IDF (0.261). Full per-prefix results in Appendix~\ref{sec:actorattack_full}.
134	
135	\paragraph{Temporal modeling.}
136	Mean pooling matches BiGRU on both DD and ActorAttack (F1 gap $\leq$ 1\,pp, overlapping CIs).
137	Turn-order shuffling produces negligible change ($|\Delta| < 1$\,pp) across both DeBERTa-v3-base 9-class (DD $\Delta$\,=\,0.000, AA $\Delta$\,=\,$-$0.009, IID $\Delta$\,=\,0.000) and RoBERTa-large 9-class (DD $\Delta$\,=\,+0.001, AA $\Delta$\,=\,$-$0.007, IID $\Delta$\,=\,0.000), confirming that detection relies on per-turn representations rather than sequential patterns.
138	Turn-order shuffling confirms this: randomizing turn order produces $\Delta < 0.01$ across all evaluation sets for domain-adapted models, while vanilla DeBERTa degrades by 8.8\,pp on DD OOD, indicating that unadapted models partially rely on positional cues that adapted representations render redundant.
139	
140	\paragraph{Summary.}
141	For base-size encoders (125--184M), domain exposure appears to be the dominant factor in our experimental setup; label correctness, granularity, temporal modeling, and domain specificity are non-factors. Classification supervision shows a complementary stability benefit (variance reduction of 4--20$\times$ observed across 3 seeds) without changing the detection ceiling.
142

_source line 6879 · 2026-05-20T09:38:25.618803+00:00_

19	\paragraph{Encoder capacity and temporal modeling.}
20	Our three encoders (125M--355M) span a 2.8$\times$ capacity range but differ in pretraining and tokenization; same-architecture scaling is needed to confirm. Mean pooling matches BiGRU (Section~\ref{sec:decomposition}), suggesting detection relies on turn-level features rather than sequential order.
21	
22	\section{Conclusion}
23	\label{sec:conclusion}
24	
25	On synthetic multi-turn data generated by a single LLM, classification-based DAPT is the most robust adaptation for base-size encoders; unsupervised pretraining closes $\sim$60\,pp on single-source data but collapses on mixed-LLM training. Fewer attacks require adaptation as model size grows, and a variance reduction of 4--20$\times$ is observed (3 seeds) on synthetic data but reverses on human data.
26	Three negative results---scrambled labels match correct, mean pooling matches BiGRU, and domain specificity is marginal---constrain which adaptation properties matter for cross-attack generalization. Lightweight continued pretraining offers a practical alternative to LLM-as-judge approaches for real-time multi-turn safety monitoring.
27	
28	\section{Limitations}

_source line 6881 · 2026-05-20T09:38:26.022315+00:00_

24	\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*, itemsep=2pt, topsep=4pt]
25	  \item \textbf{Systematic decomposition of cross-attack generalization.} Controlled ablation across thirteen encoder configurations, three OOD attack families, and two training compositions identifies classification-based domain adaptation as the most robust variant within synthetic multi-turn evaluation; unsupervised pretraining is effective on single-source data but collapses on mixed-LLM training. Three negative results are counter-intuitive: scrambled labels match correct labels, mean pooling matches BiGRU, and label granularity does not affect detection.
26	
27	  \item \textbf{Capacity-dependent observation on domain adaptation.} Three encoder scales (125M, 184M, 355M) show that the set of attacks requiring adaptation shrinks with model size; RoBERTa-large handles structurally complex attacks without any adaptation (Section~\ref{sec:cross_encoder}).

[Director 指令] [Patch] Related Work 引用补充 + bib 格式修复

**1. 必须补充的引用（按优先级）：**

**JBShield (Li et al., USENIX Security 2025, 2502.07557)**
- 基于表示层的多轮 jailbreak 检测，直接竞争对手
- 插入位置：§ Multi-turn defenses，紧接 X-Boundary 之后
- 论述角度：JBShield 使用内部表示分析，我们使用黑盒分类；JBShield 未做跨攻击族 OOD 评估

**Perez et al. 2022 (EMNLP'22, 2202.03286) — Red Teaming Language Models**

2	
3	\section{Related Work}
4	\label{sec:related_work}
5	
6	\paragraph{Multi-turn jailbreak attacks.}
7	All multi-turn jailbreak families share a persuasion substrate~\citep{zeng2024pap,hagendorff2026reasoning,li2024mhj}, yet each operationalizes it differently: Crescendo~\citep{russinovich2024crescendo} gradually escalates, FITD~\citep{fitd2024} exploits commitment-consistency bias, DD~\citep{deceptivedelight2024} combines emotional manipulation with role assignment, ActorAttack~\citep{ren2024actorattack} steers through multiple personas, and automated methods (PAIR~\citep{chao2023pair}, TAP~\citep{mehrotra2024tap}) iteratively refine adversarial prompts. \citet{wei2023jailbroken} provide a unifying taxonomy; benchmarks~\citep{mazeika2024harmbench,chao2024jailbreakbench} standardize single-turn comparison, while MultiBreak~\citep{song2026multibreak} targets multi-turn evaluation. This shared substrate with diverse surface strategies defines our evaluation: a detector must generalize across families, not merely succeed on its training distribution.
8	
9	\paragraph{Multi-turn defenses and production safety.}
10	No existing defense evaluates cross-attack transfer. Recent conversation-level systems pair fine-tuned encoders with sequence-level aggregation via GRU hierarchies (DeepContext~\citep{deepcontext2026}), turn-intention inference (BIID~\citep{tong2025biid}), graph neural networks (G-Guard~\citep{gguard2025}), or predictive generation (SafeDream~\citep{safedream2026}), yet each evaluates on a single attack family. Representation-level approaches include X-Boundary~\citep{lu2025xboundary} for multi-turn safety boundaries, white-box internal analysis~\citep{kadali2026trace}, and vision-language detection~\citep{hua2026rcs}; our work uses black-box classification. Per-turn classifiers (Llama Guard~\citep{inan2023llamaguard,metallamaguard3_2024}, ShieldGemma~\citep{zeng2024shieldgemma}, WildGuard~\citep{han2024wil
…[truncated 2473 chars]

## Latent Adversarial Detection: Adaptive Probing of LLM Activations for Multi-Turn Attack Detection

The paper was submitted on April 30, 2026, by Prashant Kulkarni.

### Key Findings:

Current defenses against prompt injection and jailbreaking operate at the text level through pattern matching, perplexity filters, and classifier-based input screening, but these approaches detect surface features rather than adversarial intent.

The paper proposes a novel defense approach: probing the target LLM's internal activations to detect adversarial intent in how the model understands an incoming interaction, not in what the attacker wrote.

## Paper Overview

The paper "Revisiting JBShield: Breaking and Rebuilding Representation-Level Jailbreak Defenses" was submitted on May 4, 2026 by authors Kemal Derya and Berk Sunar.

## Main Findings

The paper addresses the challenge of defending large language models (LLMs) against jailbreak attacks, such as Greedy Coordinate Gradient (GCG), particularly under adaptive threat models where an attacker directly targets the defense mechanism.

…[truncated 674 chars]

_source line 6969 · 2026-05-20T10:55:55.836759+00:00_

6	\paragraph{Multi-turn jailbreak attacks.}
7	All multi-turn jailbreak families share a persuasion substrate~\citep{zeng2024pap,hagendorff2026reasoning,li2024mhj}, yet each operationalizes it differently: Crescendo~\citep{russinovich2024crescendo} gradually escalates, FITD~\citep{fitd2024} exploits commitment-consistency bias, DD~\citep{deceptivedelight2024} combines emotional manipulation with role assignment, ActorAttack~\citep{ren2024actorattack} steers through multiple personas, and automated methods (PAIR~\citep{chao2023pair}, TAP~\citep{mehrotra2024tap}) iteratively refine adversarial prompts. \citet{wei2023jailbroken} provide a unifying taxonomy; benchmarks~\citep{mazeika2024harmbench,chao2024jailbreakbench} standardize single-turn comparison, while MultiBreak~\citep{song2026multibreak} targets multi-turn evaluation. This shared substrate with diverse surface strategies defines our evaluation: a detector must generalize across families, not merely succeed on its training distribution.
8	
9	\paragraph{Multi-turn defenses and production safety.}
10	No existing defense evaluates cross-attack transfer. Recent conversation-level systems pair fine-tuned encoders with sequence-level aggregation via GRU hierarchies (DeepContext~\citep{deepcontext2026}), turn-intention inference (BIID~\citep{tong2025biid}), graph neural networks (G-Guard~\citep{gguard2025}), or predictive generation (SafeDream~\citep{safedream2026}), yet each evaluates on a single attack family. Representation-level approaches include X-Boundary~\citep{lu2025xboundary} for multi-turn safety boundaries, white-box internal analysis~\citep{kadali2026trace}, and vision-language detection~\citep{hua2026rcs}; our work uses black-box classification. Per-turn classifiers (Llama Guard~\citep{inan2023llamaguard,metallamaguard3_2024}, ShieldGemma~\citep{zeng2024shieldgemma}, WildGuard~\citep{han2024wildguard}, Constitutional Classifiers~\citep{anthropic2025constitutional}) cannot model multi-turn trajectories, a
…[truncated 2361 chars]

_source line 7007 · 2026-05-20T11:38:09.872340+00:00_

[Director 指令] [Patch] 紧急修复 — 2 个 CRITICAL + 1 个 WARNING

**C1 [CRITICAL] 结论溢出第 9 页 — desk reject 风险**

§6 Conclusion 正文约 5-6 行溢出到第 9 页。EMNLP 2026 硬限制 8 页正文（Limitations/Ethics 不计入）。

修复策略（任选组合，目标：结论最后一行在第 8 页页脚内）：
- 压缩 §5 Discussion 措辞（5 段各省 1-2 行 → 收回 5-6 行）
- 或压缩 §4.7/§4.8 合并为一段
- 或将部分 §4.6 Human Data Validation 细节移入 appendix
- **不要删实质内容**，只精简措辞

**C2 [CRITICAL] 缺独立 Ethics Statement**

在 §7 Limitations 之后、References 之前添加 `\section*{Ethics Statement}`（3-5 句，从 Checklist §B 精简提取）。不计入 8 页限制。

**W3 [WARNING] 15 个未引用的 \label**

清理以下未被 \ref 引用的 label（删除不需要的，或添加缺失的交叉引用）：
`sec:setup`, `sec:stage2`, `sec:introduction`, `sec:related_work`, `sec:experiments`, `sec:conclusion`, `fig:cross_attack_bar`, `fig:cka_heatmap`, `tab:ood_perturbation`, `tab:roberta_large_lr`, `tab:tost_sensitivity`, `sec:mixed_llm`, `app:scale_validation`, `sec:cross_attack`, `sec:stage1`

修复后必须验证：编译通过 + 正文 ≤8 页（Conclusion 最后一行在第 8 页内）+ Ethics Statement 存在。不验证引用。

1	% conclusion.tex — compressed for 8-page limit
2	
3	\section{Discussion}
4	\label{sec:discussion}
5	
6	\paragraph{Topic shortcut confound.}
7	Three observations argue against a topic-level shortcut: (1) topic classification yields unstable OOD generalization (F1\,=\,0.541$\pm$0.207 on DD); (2) scrambled labels match correct labels (F1 gap $\leq$3.9\,pp); (3) MLM detects without labels (F1\,$\geq$\,0.97 at $K \leq 5$). Domain exposure, not topic classification, drives detection.
8	
9	\paragraph{Single-LLM confound.}
10	Both training and synthetic OOD data are generated by Qwen3-8B. ToxicChat confirms transfer to human data (+18--42\,pp; Table~\ref{tab:human_data}); cross-LLM experiments validate transfer when the benign distribution is held constant (Appendix~\ref{app:cross_llm}).
11	Mixed-LLM 9-class maintains DD F1\,=\,0.818$\pm$0.096 (+53\,pp over vanilla) while MLM performs at vanilla level, but AA degrades (0.504$\pm$0.107). This is condition-specific: single-source training achieves AA F1\,$\geq$\,0.91; the gap likely reflects limited per-source volume (250 per LLM).
12	
13	\paragraph{Variance reversal on human data.}
14	On synthetic OOD, classification supervision reduces variance 4--20$\times$ (3 seeds) over MLM; on ToxicChat this reverses (std\,=\,0.087 vs.\ 0.013), suggesting the benefit is condition-specific.
15	
16	\paragraph{Why does scrambled equal correct?}
17	Scrambled labels match correct labels within 3.9\,pp. Post-hoc hypotheses include regularization equivalence (any multi-class cross-entropy provides comparable structure), gradient diversity from 9 vs.\ 2 classes, and domain exposure dominance. Distinguishing these requires varying class count while holding domain exposure constant.
18	
19	\paragraph{Encoder capacity and temporal modeling.}
20	Our three encoders (125M--355M) span a 2.8$\times$ capacity range but differ in pretraining and tokenization; same-architecture scaling is needed to confirm. Mean pooling matches BiGRU and turn-order shuf
…[truncated 9294 chars]

2	
3	\section{Experiments}
4	\label{sec:experiments}
5	
6	\subsection{Setup}
7	\label{sec:setup}
8	
9	\paragraph{Data.}
10	All synthetic data is generated by Qwen3-8B~\citep{qwen3_2025}.
11	The IID dataset contains 250 Crescendo~\citep{russinovich2024crescendo} jailbreak and 250 topic-anchored benign conversations, split into training (350), validation (74), and test (76) sets with balanced class ratios (e.g., test = 38 jailbreak + 38 benign).
12	Stage~1 (DAPT) uses training + validation conversations; Stage~2 (BiGRU) trains on training, early-stops on validation, and evaluates on test.
13	OOD evaluation pairs attack-specific jailbreak conversations with the 38 IID-test benign conversations: 80 FITD~\citep{fitd2024} + 38 benign = 118 total, 80 Deceptive Delight (DD)~\citep{deceptivedelight2024} + 38 benign = 118 total, and 80 ActorAttack~\citep{ren2024actorattack} + 38 benign = 118 total.
14	No overlap exists in attack templates across families.
15	The OOD benign set is IID (same generation pipeline as training); the OOD challenge is in distinguishing novel attack families from familiar benign conversations.
16	A paraphrased version of the IID test set (BERTScore 0.92, lexical overlap 0.20) serves as the adversarial robustness benchmark (Section~\ref{sec:perturbation}).
17	For human data validation, we use ToxicChat~\citep{lin2023toxicchat} (204 jailbreak + 204 benign from real LMSYS user interactions), entirely independent of Qwen3-8B generation.
18	
19	\paragraph{Models.}
20	Seven DeBERTa-v3 variants share the same Stage-2 pipeline and differ only in Stage-1 training (Section~\ref{sec:method}).
21	Our vanilla DeBERTa+BiGRU configuration is architecturally equivalent to DeepContext~\citep{deepcontext2026}, differing only in encoder choice (DeBERTa-v3-base vs.\ BERT); it serves as our primary baseline.
22	For cross-encoder validation, we replicate vanilla, MLM, and 9-class variants on RoBERTa-base~\citep{liu2019
…[truncated 19202 chars]

1	% method.tex — D024 compressed
2	
3	\section{Method}
4	\label{sec:method}
5	
6	Cross-attack generalization could stem from domain exposure, classification supervision, label correctness, label granularity, or domain specificity. Disentangling these factors demands an architecture where exactly one component varies across conditions. DisPeD separates encoder adaptation (Stage~1) from temporal classification (Stage~2). Seven Stage-1 variants share the same training data and downstream pipeline but differ solely in their pretraining objective; each isolates a single factor (Figure~\ref{fig:architecture}).
7	
8	\subsection{Problem Formulation}
9	
10	A conversation $C = \{u_1, a_1, \ldots, u_N, a_N\}$ alternates user turns $u_i$ and assistant responses $a_i$. Given a prefix of $K$ user turns, the model predicts $y \in \{0, 1\}$ (jailbreak vs.\ benign). We restrict input to user turns because adversarial intent resides in the attacker's messages. This formulation supports early detection from partial prefixes.
11	
12	\subsection{Stage 1: Turn-Level Encoder Adaptation}
13	\label{sec:stage1}
14	
15	Each user turn $u_i$ is encoded by DeBERTa-v3-base~\citep{he2023debertav3}; the \texttt{[CLS]} embedding $h_i \in \mathbb{R}^d$ serves as the turn representation. Seven variants, summarized in Table~\ref{tab:variants}, share the same training data but differ in objective.
16

25	\midrule
26	9-class & 9-class persuasion + binary intent & full model \\
27	Binary & binary persuasion + binary intent & granularity matters \\
28	Scrambled & random 9-class + binary intent & label correctness matters \\
29	MLM-only & 15\% token masking &

29	
30	Table~\ref{tab:llm_judge} reports LLM-as-judge performance on IID and OOD data.
31	On IID Crescendo, GPT-5.5 achieves precision of 1.0 at all turn prefixes but recall ranges from only 0.105 to 0.290, resulting in F1 = 0.383 on full conversations; 30--47\% of responses are unparseable.
32	Qwen3-8B performs worse (IID F1 = 0.100).
33	On OOD data, zero-shot Qwen3-8B achieves macro F1 = 0.244 on both DD and ActorAttack by predicting nearly all conversations as benign.
34	Even with 3-shot prompting, Qwen3-8B reaches only 0.566 (DD) and 0.652 (AA), while DisPeD maintains macro F1 $\geq$ 0.972.
35	The poor performance of content-level reasoning on unseen attack types underscores the necessity of trained classifiers with domain-adapted representations.

1	% checklist.tex — ARR Responsible NLP Research Checklist
2	
3	\section*{Responsible NLP Research Checklist}
4	
5	\paragraph{A. Limitations.}
6	\begin{enumerate}[label=A\arabic*.,leftmargin=2em]
7	  \item Did you describe the limitations of your work? \textbf{Yes.} Section~\ref{sec:limitations} discusses dataset scale, lexical separability, annotation quality, OOD strategy prediction, single-seed variants, scope, and compute cost.
8	  \item Did you discuss any potential risks of your work? \textbf{Yes.} See below.
9	\end{enumerate}
10	
11	\paragraph{B. Potential Risks and Ethical Considerations.}
12	This work studies multi-turn jailbreak attacks against LLMs. While the analysis is defensive (improving detection), we acknowledge dual-use risk: the persuasion taxonomy and attack strategy analysis could inform adversarial actors. We mitigate this by: (1) using only synthetic conversations generated by an open-weight model (Qwen3-8B), not real attack data targeting production systems; (2) not releasing attack-generation prompts that could enable novel attacks; (3) focusing the contribution on detection mechanisms rather than attack techniques. All jailbreak conversations are synthetic and do not contain real harmful content. No human subjects were involved.
13	
14	\paragraph{C. Computational Experiments.}
15	\begin{enumerate}[label=C\arabic*.,leftmargin=2em]
16	  \item Did you report the number of parameters in the models used? \textbf{Yes.} DeBERTa-v3-base (184M) and RoBERTa-base (125M) in Sections~\ref{sec:method} and~\ref{sec:discussion}.
17	  \item Did you report the total amount of compute and the type of resources used? \textbf{Yes.} Under 4 GPU-hours total on a single NVIDIA A100 (40GB). Section~\ref{sec:limitations} and Appendix~\ref{sec:reproducibility}.
18	  \item Did you report the implementation details sufficient for reproducibility? \textbf{Yes.} Appendix~\ref{sec:reproducibility} provides all hyperparameters. We will release code and data upon acceptance

1	% introduction.tex — expanded for 8-page target
2	
3	\section{Introduction}
4	\label{sec:introduction}
5	
6	Multi-turn jailbreak attacks distribute adversarial intent across individually benign turns. Crescendo~\citep{russinovich2024crescendo}, Foot-in-the-Door~\citep{fitd2024}, and Deceptive Delight~\citep{deceptivedelight2024} all rely on persuasion as their core mechanism: \citet{zeng2024pap} catalogue 40 persuasion techniques across jailbreak families, and \citet{hagendorff2026reasoning} identify strategies in 97\% of successful attacks. Because individual attack turns occupy the same embedding region as benign text~\citep{bullwinkel2025repeng}, no single-turn filter can distinguish them from legitimate conversation.
7	
8	Existing defenses leave a critical gap. Production classifiers such as Llama Guard~\citep{inan2023llamaguard}, WildGuard~\citep{han2024wildguard}, and ShieldGemma~\citep{zeng2024shieldgemma} operate on individual turns and therefore miss manipulation that unfolds across exchanges. Multi-turn defenses have begun to model conversation-level structure: DeepContext~\citep{deepcontext2026} pairs fine-tuned BERT with a multi-layer GRU, BIID~\citep{tong2025biid} infers turn-level intentions, and G-Guard~\citep{gguard2025} applies graph neural networks. These systems achieve strong in-distribution performance, but each is evaluated on a single attack family. None answers the central question: \emph{what representation properties enable a detector to generalize across attack families it has never seen?}
9	
10	The simplest hypothesis is that pretrained language understanding already suffices. It does not, at least at base model scale. A vanilla DeBERTa-v3~\citep{he2021deberta} with temporal aggregation (cf.\ DeepContext) drops from F1\,=\,1.000 in-distribution to 0.312 on out-of-distribution (OOD) Deceptive Delight. LLM-as-judge approaches fare no better: GPT-5.5 reaches only 0.191--0.449 F1 with 30--47\% unparseable responses (Appendix~\ref{sec:llm_ju
…[truncated 3505 chars]

_source line 7047 · 2026-05-20T11:43:27.226477+00:00_

60	
61	\paragraph{Topic variant anomaly.}
62	Topic classification occasionally approaches domain-adapted performance on ActorAttack (0.625 vs.\ MLM 0.893) despite using misaligned labels. This reflects domain exposure from processing jailbreak text, not the topic objective itself; extreme variance (std\,=\,0.141--0.207) confirms that misaligned supervision amplifies initialization sensitivity.
63	
64	\paragraph{Scope.}
65	Only persuasion-mediated multi-turn attacks fall within scope. Single-turn token-level attacks~\citep{zou2023gcg}, non-persuasive multi-turn strategies, and attacks targeting modalities other than text are not addressed. The user-only input assumption (excluding assistant responses) may miss information in attacks where the model's own responses contribute to the escalation trajectory.
66	
67	\paragraph{Single-turn transfer.}
68	ToxicChat jailbreaks are exclusively single-turn, while all training data consists of multi-turn conversations (5--14 turns). ToxicChat performance therefore reflects zero-shot single-turn transfer, not standard multi-turn OOD evaluation. The temporal aggregator can learn sequence length as a spurious feature: doubling the training corpus from 500 to 1{,}000 conversations drops 9-class ToxicChat macro F1 from 0.596 to 0.341$\pm$0.004, while multi-turn OOD detection remains stable (DD\,=\,0.889, ActorAttack\,=\,0.913). This length-dependent fragility confirms that the architecture targets multi-turn detection; single-turn robustness requires architectural changes beyond this work's scope.
69	
70	\paragraph{Computation cost.}
71	Stage~1 continued pretraining requires approximately 30 minutes on a single A100 GPU per variant. Stage~2 training completes in under 5 minutes. Total pipeline cost for all seven variants is under 4 GPU-hours, making the approach accessible for replication.
72

1	% conclusion.tex — compressed for 8-page limit
2	
3	\section{Discussion}
4	\label{sec:discussion}
5	
6	\paragraph{Topic shortcut confound.}
7	Three observations argue against a topic-level shortcut: topic classification yields unstable OOD generalization (F1\,=\,0.541$\pm$0.207), scrambled labels match correct within 3.9\,pp, and MLM detects without labels (F1\,$\geq$\,0.97 at $K \leq 5$). Domain exposure drives detection.
8	
9	\paragraph{Single-LLM confound.}
10	Both training and OOD data use Qwen3-8B. ToxicChat confirms human transfer (+18--42\,pp; Table~\ref{tab:human_data}); cross-LLM experiments validate transfer with constant benign distribution (Appendix~\ref{app:cross_llm}).
11	Mixed-LLM 9-class maintains DD F1\,=\,0.818$\pm$0.096 (+53\,pp over vanilla) while MLM collapses; AA degrades to 0.504$\pm$0.107, likely reflecting limited per-source volume (250 per LLM).
12	
13	\paragraph{Variance reversal on human data.}
14	Classification supervision reduces variance 4--20$\times$ over MLM on synthetic OOD (3 seeds); on ToxicChat this reverses (std\,=\,0.087 vs.\ 0.013).
15	
16	\paragraph{Why does scrambled equal correct?}
17	Scrambled labels match correct within 3.9\,pp. Post-hoc hypotheses---regularization equivalence, gradient diversity, domain exposure dominance---require varying class count with constant domain exposure to distinguish.
18	
19	\paragraph{Encoder capacity and temporal modeling.}
20	Our three encoders (125M--355M) span a 2.8$\times$ capacity range but differ in pretraining and tokenization; same-architecture scaling is needed. Mean pooling matches BiGRU and shuffling produces $|\Delta| < 1$\,pp across encoders (Section~\ref{sec:decomposition}), confirming order-invariant detection.
21	
22	\section{Conclusion}
23	
24	On single-LLM synthetic data, classification-based DAPT is the most robust adaptation for base-size encoders; unsupervised pretraining closes $\sim$60\,pp on single-source data but collapses under mixed-LLM training. Fewer attacks r

_source line 7107 · 2026-05-20T11:49:00.692937+00:00_

220	\subsection{Perturbation Analysis}
221	\label{sec:perturbation}
222	
223	Under adversarial paraphrasing (BERTScore 0.92), classification-supervised variants achieve zero degradation on IID and OOD DD (Table~\ref{tab:ood_perturbation}) while non-adapted models degrade by $\sim$12\,pp ($p < 0.001$). On ActorAttack, DAPT variants degrade (9-class $\Delta$\,=\,$-$20.4$\pm$13.5\,pp) but maintain a 52\,pp advantage over vanilla (0.768 vs.\ 0.248). Full results in Appendix~\ref{sec:perturbation_full}.
224	
225	%----------------------------------------------------------------------
226	\subsection{Strategy Trajectory Analysis}
227	\label{sec:strategy}
228	
229	The 9-class taxonomy provides interpretable characterization of known attacks ($\kappa$=0.78, $n$=50; Section~\ref{sec:method}); transition matrices reveal distinct orchestration patterns per family (Appendix~\ref{sec:strategy_full}).
230	On unseen attacks, strategy prediction degrades to near-random ($\kappa$=0.126 on OOD DD); the taxonomy should not be viewed as an OOD interpretability tool. The taxonomy's value is twofold: (1) as a training signal---even scrambled labels produce equivalent detection, indicating that the classification task structure matters more than label semantics; (2) as an IID characterization tool for known attack families. Detection operates through domain-adapted \texttt{[CLS]} embeddings, not through strategy classification accuracy.
231

_source line 7121 · 2026-05-20T11:50:03.548325+00:00_

278	Table~\ref{tab:scale_validation} validates robustness to dataset scale by doubling the training corpus from 500 to 1{,}000 conversations with new topic seeds. The hierarchy 9-class $\gg$ MLM $\approx$ vanilla holds at 2$\times$ scale: classification-based DAPT maintains 40--56\,pp advantage over vanilla, while MLM provides no improvement over vanilla on either attack family (DD: 0.338 vs.\ 0.326; AA: 0.351 vs.\ 0.508).
279	
280	\section{RoBERTa-large DAPT Learning Rate Control}
281	\label{app:roberta_large_lr}
282	
283	\begin{table}[ht]
284	\centering
285	\footnotesize
286	\caption{RoBERTa-large (355M) DAPT learning rate control on OOD Full (3-seed mean$\pm$std). Reducing the learning rate worsens both DD and AA, ruling out over-adaptation as the cause of the regression in Table~\ref{tab:cross_encoder}.}
287	\label{tab:roberta_large_lr}
288	\begin{tabular}{@{}lcc@{}}
289	\toprule
290	\textbf{Config} & DD Full & AA Full \\
291	\midrule
292	Vanilla (no DAPT) & .927$\pm$.036 & 1.000$\pm$.000 \\
293	9-class, LR=$2 \times 10^{-5}$ & .987$\pm$.004 & .946$\pm$.057 \\
294	9-class, LR=$1 \times 10^{-5}$ & .948$\pm$.034 & .781$\pm$.054 \\
295	\bottomrule
296	\end{tabular}
297	\end{table}
298	
299	Reducing the DAPT learning rate from $2 \times 10^{-5}$ to $1 \times 10^{-5}$ worsens both DD and AA performance, which rules out over-adaptation as the cause of the regression observed in Table~\ref{tab:cross_encoder}. An MLM-only variant (without classification) produces the same pattern (AA Full = 0.874$\pm$0.014); representation drift from continued pretraining, not classification-specific bias, is the underlying cause. Domain-adaptive fine-tuning narrows the representation space of a sufficiently large encoder and reduces its inherent OOD generalization ability.
300	
301	\section{TOST Equivalence Sensitivity Analysis}
302	\label{app:tost_sensitivity}

639

Conclusion

On single-LLM synthetic data, classification-based
DAPT is the most robust adaptation for base-size
encoders; unsupervised pretraining closes ∼60 pp
on single-source data but collapses under mixedLLM training. Fewer attacks require adaptation as
model size grows; variance reduction of 4–20× (3
seeds) on synthetic data reverses on human data.
Three negative results—scrambled labels match
correct, temporal order is irrelevant, and domain
specificity is marginal—constrain which properties
matter. Lightweight continued pretraining offers a
practical alternative to LLM-as-judge for real-time
multi-turn safety monitoring.

**新数据**：DeBERTa-v3-base 9-class E2E fine-tuning（解冻 encoder）vs frozen probing：

| 指标 | Frozen Probing | E2E Fine-tuning | Δ |
|------|---------------|----------------|---|
| IID | 1.000±0.000 | 1.000±0.000 | 0.000 |
| DD OOD | 0.994±0.005 | 1.000±0.000 | +0.006 |
| AA OOD | 0.939±0.047 | 0.997±0.005 | +0.058 |

**写入位置和方式**：

1. **Limitations "Frozen encoder assumption" 段落**：补充一句引用 exp18 数据，例如："End-to-end fine-tuning closes this gap to ≤5.8pp on the hardest OOD set (ActorAttack F1: 0.997 vs 0.939), confirming that frozen probing captures the majority of discriminative information while preserving analytical interpretability."

2. **Appendix 新增表格**：Frozen vs E2E 完整对比表（含 3 个评估集 × 3 seeds）

3. **Discussion "Encoder capacity" 段落**：如果有空间，可加一句 "an end-to-end fine-tuning upper bound (Appendix X) shows frozen probing sacrifices ≤5.8pp on the hardest evaluation"

**关键叙事**：frozen probing 是方法论选择（允许 interpretability），E2E 作为 upper bound 证明代价可控。不要框架成 "frozen 不如 E2E"。

_source line 7151 · 2026-05-20T12:38:42.926838+00:00_

14	Classification supervision reduces variance 4--20$\times$ over MLM on synthetic OOD (3 seeds); on ToxicChat this reverses (std\,=\,0.087 vs.\ 0.013).
15	
16	\paragraph{Encoder capacity and temporal modeling.}
17	Our three encoders (125M--355M) span a 2.8$\times$ capacity range but differ in pretraining and tokenization; same-architecture scaling is needed. Mean pooling matches BiGRU and shuffling produces $|\Delta| < 1$\,pp across encoders (Section~\ref{sec:decomposition}), confirming order-invariant detection.
18	
19	\section{Conclusion}
20	
21	On single-LLM synthetic data, classification-based DAPT is the most robust adaptation for base-size encoders; unsupervised pretraining closes $\sim$60\,pp on single-source data but collapses under mixed-LLM training. Fewer attacks require adaptation as model size grows; variance reduction of 4--20$\times$ (3 seeds) on synthetic data reverses on human data. Three negative results---scrambled labels match correct, temporal order is irrelevant, and domain specificity is marginal---constrain which properties matter. Lightweight continued pretraining offers a practical alternative to LLM-as-judge for real-time multi-turn safety monitoring.
22	
23	\section{Limitations}
24	\label{sec:limitations}
25

_source line 7153 · 2026-05-20T12:38:43.529706+00:00_

33	The Stage-1 encoder is frozen during Stage-2 training, following the probing methodology of \citet{conneau2018probing} and the feature-based paradigm of \citet{devlin2019bert}. This design isolates Stage-1 representation quality but may underestimate full-pipeline performance: unfreezing the encoder would allow end-to-end adaptation that could close remaining OOD gaps, at the cost of confounding representation quality with downstream optimization.
34	
35	\paragraph{Binary detection head.}
36	All classification variants include both a binary jailbreak detection head and a strategy classification head. Binary vs.\ 9-class performance equivalence (F1 gap $\leq$3.9\,pp) suggests the binary head alone is not the critical differentiator, but a strategy-only variant (without binary head) would be needed to fully disentangle their contributions.
37	
38	\paragraph{Dataset scale and diversity.}
39	The training set uses synthetic conversations generated by Qwen3-8B. We validate robustness to dataset scale by doubling the training corpus from 500 to 1{,}000 conversations (Appendix~\ref{app:scale_validation}): classification-based DAPT maintains strong OOD generalization (9-class DD F1\,=\,0.889$\pm$0.041, ActorAttack\,=\,0.913$\pm$0.041), and the hierarchy 9-class $\gg$ MLM $\approx$ vanilla holds (DAPT advantage 40--56\,pp over vanilla). The perturbation evaluation ($n$=76) remains underpowered; tier boundaries in Table~\ref{tab:perturbation} correspond to 1--3 misclassifications. All multi-turn data is synthetic. ToxicChat (human-authored, single-turn) confirms transfer across data sources (+18--42\,pp over vanilla; Section~\ref{sec:human_data}), but whether the full evidence hierarchy generalizes to human-generated multi-turn attacks is open.
40	Cross-LLM validation with Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct as the target model reveals that jailbreak detection transfers across LLMs (F1 recovers to 0.80--1.00 when the benign distribution is held constant), but the benign baseline is LLM-s

330	
331	\paragraph{Strategy annotation.}
332	Each user turn is annotated by Qwen3-8B using a structured prompt that provides definitions and two examples for each of the nine strategy categories. The prompt instructs the model to output a single category label. We plan to release all generation and annotation prompts, the full dataset, and training code upon acceptance.
333	
334	\paragraph{Adversarial paraphrasing.}
335	The paraphrased test set is generated by instructing Qwen3-8B to rephrase each user turn while preserving the communicative intent. The resulting paraphrases have mean BERTScore 0.92 and mean lexical overlap 0.20 with the originals. These metrics confirm substantial surface-level variation.
336

These metrics confirm substantial surface-level variation.\n\n\\section{End-to-End Fine-Tuning Upper Bound}\n\\label{app:e2e}\n\n\\begin{table}[ht]\n\\centering\n\\footnotesize\n\\caption{Frozen probing vs.\\ end-to-end (E2E) fine-tuning for DeBERTa-v3-base 9-class (3-seed mean$\\pm$std). 24	F1 for binary jailbreak detection at prefix $K$=2 and full conversations (full per-$K$ results in Appendix~\ref{sec:full_prefix}).

26	For key pairwise comparisons, we report BCa bootstrap 95\% confidence intervals (10,000 resamples over individual predictions pooled across seeds) and paired permutation tests (10,000 permutations). Bootstrap means may differ slightly from seed-averaged F1 due to the nonlinearity of F1 aggregation.
27	All standard deviations are computed over 3 random seeds using the population formula ($n$ divisor); Bessel-corrected estimates ($n{-}1$ divisor) are $\approx$22\% larger but do not alter any significance conclusions.
28	Stage-1: learning rate $2 \times 10^{-5}$, batch 16, 4 epochs, MLM masking 15\%.
29	Stage-2: two-layer MLP, 20 epochs.

### Tool result

_source line 7217 · 2026-05-20T12:44:44.490226+00:00_

108	\paragraph{Domain exposure is the primary mechanism.}
109	MLM bridges $\sim$60\,pp from vanilla (0.312$\pm$0.026 $\to$ 0.914$\pm$0.115) without labels; this supports the DAPT interpretation~\citep{gururangan2020dontstop}. TF-IDF collapse on ActorAttack (0.261 vs.\ 9-class 0.972) confirms that domain-adapted representations capture features beyond lexical overlap.
110	All three classification-supervised variants converge to equivalent detection (F1 = 0.997--1.000); McNemar's test finds no significant pairwise differences (all $p > 0.13$; $\leq$7 discordant pairs limit power), and paired permutation tests confirm equivalence ($p \geq 0.40$). TOST equivalence tests (margin = 5\,pp) confirm statistical equivalence on DD (all $p < 0.01$, 90\% CI within $\pm$0.05), robust to $\delta$=2\,pp ($p$=0.035; Table~\ref{tab:tost_sensitivity}). On ActorAttack, the mean gap is 2.1\,pp (within noise at $n$=3) but TOST is underpowered (SE\,=\,0.045) to confirm formal equivalence; descriptively similar but formally inconclusive. Label quality and granularity are non-factors for detection.
111	Classification supervision reduces cross-seed variance 4--20$\times$ over MLM on synthetic OOD (std 0.005 vs.\ 0.115 on DD; observed across 3 seeds, suggestive but not conclusive). This stability benefit does not transfer to human-authored data, where classification-supervised variants exhibit the highest variance and pure MLM the lowest (Section~\ref{sec:human_data}).
112	Appendix~\ref{app:tsne} provides t-SNE visualizations confirming that domain-adapted variants produce tighter, more separable embedding clusters than the vanilla baseline.
113	
114	\paragraph{Domain specificity is marginal at full length but meaningful for early detection.}
115	Wikipedia MLM (0.904$\pm$0.115) nearly matches jailbreak-domain MLM at full length (1\,pp gap), though jailbreak-domain text provides an 8\,pp advantage at early prefixes ($K$=2: 0.993 vs.\ 0.911).

61	On structurally complex ActorAttack, TF-IDF collapses to F1 = 0.261 (precision = 1.0, recall = 0.15), while 9-class maintains 0.972$\pm$0.026: a 71\,pp gap. Lexical methods cannot capture multi-persona role-playing attack structure. This establishes a ``difficulty threshold'' for lexical methods parallel to that of vanilla encoders: both succeed on DD and fail on ActorAttack (Section~\ref{sec:limitations}).
62	Bootstrap CIs confirm non-overlapping intervals (9-class DD Full [0.943, 1.000] vs.\ vanilla [0.202, 0.341]; all permutation $p < 0.0001$). FITD is non-discriminative (all variants F1\,$\geq$\,0.990; Section~\ref{sec:limitations}).

_source line 7261 · 2026-05-20T12:47:37.751279+00:00_

32	\paragraph{Frozen encoder assumption.}
33	The Stage-1 encoder is frozen during Stage-2 training, following the probing methodology of \citet{conneau2018probing} and the feature-based paradigm of \citet{devlin2019bert}. This design isolates Stage-1 representation quality but may underestimate full-pipeline performance: unfreezing the encoder would allow end-to-end adaptation that could close remaining OOD gaps, at the cost of confounding representation quality with downstream optimization. End-to-end fine-tuning closes this gap to $\leq$5.8\,pp on the hardest OOD set (ActorAttack F1\,=\,0.997$\pm$0.005 vs.\ 0.939$\pm$0.047 frozen; Table~\ref{tab:e2e}), confirming that frozen probing captures the majority of discriminative information while preserving analytical interpretability.

### Tool result

_source line 7263 · 2026-05-20T12:47:38.404330+00:00_

39	The training set uses synthetic conversations generated by Qwen3-8B. We validate robustness to dataset scale by doubling the training corpus from 500 to 1{,}000 conversations (Appendix~\ref{app:scale_validation}): classification-based DAPT maintains strong OOD generalization (9-class DD F1\,=\,0.889$\pm$0.041, ActorAttack\,=\,0.913$\pm$0.041), and the hierarchy 9-class $\gg$ MLM $\approx$ vanilla holds (DAPT advantage 40--56\,pp over vanilla). The perturbation evaluation ($n$=76) remains underpowered; tier boundaries in Table~\ref{tab:perturbation} correspond to 1--3 misclassifications. All multi-turn data is synthetic. ToxicChat (human-authored, single-turn) confirms transfer across data sources (+18--42\,pp over vanilla; Section~\ref{sec:human_data}), but whether the full evidence hierarchy generalizes to human-generated multi-turn attacks is open.
40	Cross-LLM validation with Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct as the target model reveals that jailbreak detection transfers across LLMs (F1 recovers to 0.80--1.00 when the benign distribution is held constant), but the benign baseline is LLM-specific: replacing Qwen benign text with Llama benign text causes false-positive rates to spike (F1 drops to 0.37--0.43). A per-class error decomposition (Appendix~\ref{app:cross_llm}, Table~\ref{tab:cross_llm_error}) confirms that true positive rates are identical across benign conditions; the F1 collapse is driven entirely by false positives on unfamiliar benign text. The residual cross-LLM degradation on structurally complex attacks (ActorAttack F1 = 0.80 vs.\ 0.997 in-distribution) indicates that some jailbreak features remain source-specific. Why MLM effectiveness is LLM-specific remains an open question; one hypothesis is that different LLMs produce stylistically distinct text whose surface patterns MLM captures via token co-occurrence, while classification supervision extracts features that generalize across styles. Mixed-LLM training partially resolves this gap: classification-bas

[Director 指令] [Patch] exp19 WildChat FPR 写入论文 + Conclusion 措辞修正

**背景**：exp19 在 226 条 WildChat 真实 benign 对话上测试 FPR，所有变体均极高（9-class 68.1%，MLM 44.5%，vanilla 48.1%）。论文必须诚实披露并弱化 deployment claim。

**1. Appendix 新增段落 "Real-World False Positive Rate"**

展示 WildChat FPR 表格（3 variants × 3 seeds），加一段分析：
- 226 条 WildChat 多轮 benign 对话（GPT-3.5 用户，avg 7.4 turns）
- 所有变体 FPR 均远超可部署水平，9-class 最高（68.1%）
- 根因：合成训练 benign 与真实用户对话的分布偏移
- 与 cross-LLM FP spike（Table A.7）一致：benign 侧需要 per-domain calibration
- 9-class 最差 → domain adaptation 加剧了对训练 benign 分布的依赖

**2. Limitations "OOD benign distribution" 段落引用 Appendix**

补充一句引用 WildChat FPR 数据，说明在真实 benign 数据上 calibration 的必要性。

**3. Conclusion 措辞修正（关键）**

当前结论最后一句："Lightweight continued pretraining offers a practical alternative to LLM-as-judge for real-time multi-turn safety monitoring."

修改为："Lightweight continued pretraining offers a practical alternative to LLM-as-judge for real-time multi-turn safety monitoring **when calibrated to the deployment domain's benign distribution** (Appendix X)."

**页数**：主文 ≤8 页不变（新内容在 Appendix + Limitations 引用只加几个词 + Conclusion 加半句限定）。不验证引用。

1	% conclusion.tex — compressed for 8-page limit
2	
3	\section{Discussion}
4	\label{sec:discussion}
5	
6	\paragraph{Topic shortcut confound.}
7	Three observations argue against a topic-level shortcut: topic classification yields unstable OOD generalization (F1\,=\,0.541$\pm$0.207), scrambled labels match correct within 3.9\,pp, and MLM detects without labels (F1\,$\geq$\,0.97 at $K \leq 5$). Domain exposure drives detection. Why scrambled labels match correct is open; hypotheses---regularization equivalence, gradient diversity, domain exposure dominance---require varying class count with constant domain exposure to distinguish.
8	
9	\paragraph{Single-LLM confound.}
10	Both training and OOD data use Qwen3-8B. ToxicChat confirms human transfer (+18--42\,pp; Table~\ref{tab:human_data}); cross-LLM experiments validate transfer with constant benign distribution (Appendix~\ref{app:cross_llm}).
11	Mixed-LLM 9-class maintains DD F1\,=\,0.818$\pm$0.096 (+53\,pp over vanilla) while MLM collapses; AA degrades to 0.504$\pm$0.107, likely reflecting limited per-source volume (250 per LLM).
12	
13	\paragraph{Variance reversal on human data.}
14	Classification supervision reduces variance 4--20$\times$ over MLM on synthetic OOD (3 seeds); on ToxicChat this reverses (std\,=\,0.087 vs.\ 0.013).
15	
16	\paragraph{Encoder capacity and temporal modeling.}
17	Our three encoders (125M--355M) span a 2.8$\times$ capacity range but differ in pretraining and tokenization; same-architecture scaling is needed. Mean pooling matches BiGRU and shuffling produces $|\Delta| < 1$\,pp across encoders (Section~\ref{sec:decomposition}), confirming order-invariant detection.
18	
19	\section{Conclusion}
20	
21	On single-LLM synthetic data, classification-based DAPT is the most robust adaptation for base-size encoders; unsupervised pretraining closes $\sim$60\,pp on single-source data but collapses under mixed-LLM training. Fewer attacks require adaptation as model size grows; variance reduction of 4--20$\

30	Table~\ref{tab:llm_judge} reports LLM-as-judge performance on IID and OOD data.
31	On IID Crescendo, GPT-5.5 achieves precision of 1.0 at all turn prefixes but recall ranges from only 0.105 to 0.290, resulting in F1 = 0.383 on full conversations; 30--47\% of responses are unparseable.
32	Qwen3-8B performs worse (IID F1 = 0.100).
33	On OOD data, zero-shot Qwen3-8B achieves macro F1 = 0.244 on both DD and ActorAttack by predicting nearly all conversations as benign.
34	Even with 3-shot prompting, Qwen3-8B reaches only 0.566 (DD) and 0.652 (AA), while DisPeD maintains macro F1 $\geq$ 0.972.
35	The poor performance of content-level reasoning on unseen attack types underscores the necessity of trained classifiers with domain-adapted representations.

1	WildChat Hard Negatives FPR Evaluation (exp19)
2	
3	Dataset: 226 benign conversations from WildChat (filtered: multi-turn, high drift)
4	Average turns per conversation: 7.4
5	Checkpoints: exp17 (DeBERTa-v3-base, 1000-sample dataset)
6	
7	FPR (False Positive Rate = % benign incorrectly classified as jailbreak):
8	
9	9class:
10	  seed42:  FPR = 66.8%  (151/226 misclassified)
11	  seed123: FPR = 61.1%  (138/226 misclassified)
12	  seed456: FPR = 76.5%  (173/226 misclassified)
13	  mean:    FPR = 68.1 +/- 6.4%
14	
15	mlm:
16	  seed42:  FPR = 36.7%  (83/226 misclassified)
17	  seed123: FPR = 64.6%  (146/226 misclassified)
18	  seed456: FPR = 32.3%  (73/226 misclassified)
19	  mean:    FPR = 44.5 +/- 14.3%
20	
21	vanilla:
22	  seed42:  FPR = 47.3%  (107/226 misclassified)
23	  seed123: FPR = 42.5%  (96/226 misclassified)
24	  seed456: FPR = 54.4%  (123/226 misclassified)
25	  mean:    FPR = 48.1 +/- 4.9%
26	
27	Key finding: All three model variants have unacceptably high FPR on real-world WildChat benign conversations, confirming significant distribution shift between LLM-generated training benign data and real user conversations. 9-class is worst (68%), likely because the multi-task heads overfit to stylistic patterns of the training data.

Domain adaptation \\emph{increases} FPR relative to vanilla (+20\\,pp for 9-class), indicating that continued pretraining amplifies dependence on the training benign distribution. This is consistent with the cross-LLM false positive spike (Table~\\ref{tab:cross_llm_error}): models trained on synthetic Qwen3-8B benign text implicitly treat unfamiliar conversational styles as jailbreak indicators. High MLM seed variance (std\\,=\\,14.3\\%) suggests that unsupervised pretraining is particularly unstable under benign distribution shift. Deployment requires per-domain benign calibration or mixed-source benign training data."

35	\paragraph{Binary detection head.}
36	All classification variants include both a binary jailbreak detection head and a strategy classification head. Binary vs.\ 9-class performance equivalence (F1 gap $\leq$3.9\,pp) suggests the binary head alone is not the critical differentiator, but a strategy-only variant (without binary head) would be needed to fully disentangle their contributions.
37	
38	\paragraph{Dataset scale and diversity.}
39	The training set uses synthetic conversations generated by Qwen3-8B. We validate robustness to dataset scale by doubling the training corpus from 500 to 1{,}000 conversations (Appendix~\ref{app:scale_validation}): classification-based DAPT maintains strong OOD generalization (9-class DD F1\,=\,0.889$\pm$0.041, ActorAttack\,=\,0.913$\pm$0.041), and the hierarchy 9-class $\gg$ MLM $\approx$ vanilla holds (DAPT advantage 40--56\,pp over vanilla). The perturbation evaluation ($n$=76) remains underpowered; tier boundaries in Table~\ref{tab:perturbation} correspond to 1--3 misclassifications. All multi-turn data is synthetic. ToxicChat (human-authored, single-turn) confirms transfer across data sources (+18--42\,pp over vanilla; Section~\ref{sec:human_data}), but whether the full evidence hierarchy generalizes to human-generated multi-turn attacks is open.
40	Cross-LLM validation with Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct as the target model reveals that jailbreak detection transfers across LLMs (F1 recovers to 0.80--1.00 when the benign distribution is held constant), but the benign baseline is LLM-specific: replacing Qwen benign text with Llama benign text causes false-positive rates to spike (F1 drops to 0.37--0.43). A per-class error decomposition (Appendix~\ref{app:cross_llm}, Table~\ref{tab:cross_llm_error}) confirms that true positive rates are identical across benign conditions; the F1 collapse is driven entirely by false positives on unfamiliar benign text. The residual cross-LLM degradation on structurally complex attacks (ActorAttack F1 = 0
